Existing unified policies either share one action representation across <sub>nav</sub>i<sub>ga</sub>ti<sub>on an</sub>d <sub>man</sub>i<sub>pu</sub>l<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>on or separa</sub>t<sub>e</sub> th<sub>e</sub> t<sub>wo on</sub>l<sub>y</sub> i<sub>ns</sub>id<sub>e</sub> th<sub>e ac</sub>ti<sub>on mo</sub>d<sub>u</sub>l<sub>e, an</sub>d th<sub>ey</sub> l<sub>earn w</sub>h<sub>ere</sub> t<sub>o s</sub>t<sub>op on</sub>l<sub>y</sub> i<sub>mp</sub>li<sub>c</sub>itl<sub>y</sub> f<sub>rom cos</sub>tl<sub>y</sub> complete trajectories. Without such data, a <sub>p</sub>olic<sub>y</sub> ma<sub>y</sub> reach the tar<sub>g</sub>et but sto<sub>p</sub> at an unsuitable <sub>p</sub>osition or headin<sub>g</sub>, and the subse<sub>q</sub>uent mani<sub>p</sub>ulation then fails [36]. To address the first challenge, we propose UniWAM, a mixed-stream world-action model with separate navigation and <sub>man</sub>i<sub>pu</sub>l<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>on s</sub>t<sub>reams.</sub> E<sub>ac</sub>h <sub>s</sub>t<sub>ream</sub> h<sub>as</sub> it<sub>s own v</sub>i<sub>sua</sub>l i<sub>npu</sub>t<sub>s, ac</sub>ti<sub>on enco</sub>d<sub>er, an</sub>d <sub>ou</sub>t<sub>pu</sub>t h<sub>ea</sub>d <sub>over s</sub>h<sub>are</sub>d <sub>v</sub>id<sub>eo an</sub>d <sub>ac</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> DiT<sub>s.</sub> Th<sub>e</sub> t<sub>wo s</sub>t<sub>reams are samp</sub>l<sub>e</sub>d i<sub>n</sub>d<sub>epen</sub>d<sub>en</sub>tl<sub>y an</sub>d <sub>pac</sub>k<sub>e</sub>d <sub>as separa</sub>t<sub>e</sub> b<sub>a</sub>t<sub>c</sub>h <sub>rows w</sub>ith<sub>ou</sub>t <sub>cross-row a</sub>tt<sub>en</sub>ti<sub>on,</sub> <sub>so eac</sub>h <sub>samp</sub>l<sub>e superv</sub>i<sub>ses on</sub>l<sub>y</sub> it<sub>s own s</sub>t<sub>ream.</sub> N<sub>av</sub>i<sub>ga</sub>ti<sub>on-on</sub>l<sub>y, man</sub>i<sub>pu</sub>l<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>on-on</sub>l<sub>y, an</sub>d <sub>mo</sub>bil<sub>e-man</sub>i<sub>pu</sub>l<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>a can</sub> <sub>a</sub>ll b<sub>e use</sub>d di<sub>rec</sub>tl<sub>y, an</sub>d <sub>e</sub>ith<sub>er s</sub>t<sub>ream runs a</sub>l<sub>one or</sub> b<sub>o</sub>th <sub>run</sub> i<sub>n one</sub> b<sub>a</sub>t<sub>c</sub>h <sub>a</sub>t d<sub>ep</sub>l<sub>oymen</sub>t<sub>.</sub>

To address the second challenge, we introduce the Manipulation Anchor Pose (MAP), the base position and heading at <sub>w</sub>hi<sub>c</sub>h <sub>nav</sub>i<sub>ga</sub>ti<sub>on s</sub>h<sub>ou</sub>ld <sub>en</sub>d f<sub>or man</sub>i<sub>pu</sub>l<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>on.</sub> U<sub>n</sub>lik<sub>e</sub> fl<sub>oor a</sub>f<sub>or</sub>d<sub>ances, can</sub>did<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e</sub> b<sub>ase p</sub>l<sub>acemen</sub>t<sub>s, or pose pre</sub>f<sub>erences</sub> learned from rollouts [6, 8, 43, 45], MAP is an ex<sub>p</sub>licit tar<sub>g</sub>et that can be <sub>g</sub>enerated automaticall<sub>y</sub> and su<sub>p</sub>ervises the entire approach trajectory. We build MAP-Data from large-scale indoor 3D scenes with an automated pipeline, yielding <sub>over</sub> 1<sub>.</sub>5 <sub>m</sub>illi<sub>on nav</sub>i<sub>ga</sub>ti<sub>on ep</sub>i<sub>so</sub>d<sub>es an</sub>d 7<sub>,</sub>500 h<sub>ours anno</sub>t<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d <sub>w</sub>ith <sub>per-</sub>f<sub>rame</sub> t<sub>arge</sub>t b<sub>oun</sub>di<sub>ng</sub> b<sub>oxes an</sub>d i<sub>mage-p</sub>l<sub>ane</sub> MAP coordinates. We hold out a subset of its targets as MAP-Bench for evaluating MAP navigation. Because the <sub>s</sub>t<sub>reams are samp</sub>l<sub>e</sub>d i<sub>n</sub>d<sub>epen</sub>d<sub>en</sub>tl<sub>y,</sub> U<sub>n</sub>iWAM t<sub>ra</sub>i<sub>ns</sub> it<sub>s nav</sub>i<sub>ga</sub>ti<sub>on s</sub>t<sub>ream</sub> di<sub>rec</sub>tl<sub>y on</sub> MAP<sub>-</sub>D<sub>a</sub>t<sub>a, w</sub>hi<sub>c</sub>h <sub>superv</sub>i<sub>ses w</sub>h<sub>ere</sub> to stop for mobile manipulation explicitly and at scale. Together with projected end-efector trajectories for manipulation, the boundin<sub>g</sub> boxes and MAP coordinates serve as ima<sub>g</sub>e-<sub>p</sub>lane auxiliar<sub>y</sub> tar<sub>g</sub>ets for the two streams [25, 37].

O<sub>ur con</sub>t<sub>r</sub>ib<sub>u</sub>ti<sub>ons are:</sub>

• We <sub>p</sub>ro<sub>p</sub>ose UniWAM<sub>,</sub> a mixed-stream world-action model that learns navi<sub>g</sub>ation and mani<sub>p</sub>ulation as two inde<sub>p</sub>endentl<sub>y</sub> <sub>samp</sub>l<sub>e</sub>d <sub>s</sub>t<sub>reams</sub> <sub>over</sub> <sub>a</sub> <sub>s</sub>h<sub>are</sub>d b<sub>ac</sub>kb<sub>one.</sub>

Unified mobile-manipulation models. Manipulation-oriented navigation. Unlike semantic navigation, which only requires reaching the vicinity of a tar<sub>g</sub>et [15, 42], mani<sub>p</sub>ulation-oriented navi<sub>g</sub>ation must find a base <sub>p</sub>osition and headin<sub>g</sub> that su<sub>pp</sub>ort the next interaction. Existin<sub>g</sub> methods fall into three <sub>g</sub>rou<sub>p</sub>s. The first searches for feasible base <sub>p</sub>lacements from arm reachabilit<sub>y</sub> [26]. The second learns mani<sub>p</sub>ulation-read<sub>y</sub> locations from labeled or executed data, includin<sub>g</sub> floor afordance ma<sub>p</sub>s [43, 45], <sub>g</sub>ras<sub>p</sub>-aware base <sub>p</sub>lacement [8], and <sub>p</sub>ose <sub>p</sub>references learned from mani<sub>p</sub>ulation rollouts [6]. The third infers the base <sub>p</sub>ose at test time b<sub>y</sub> o<sub>p</sub>timizin<sub>g</sub> com<sub>p</sub>atibilit<sub>y</sub> with a mani<sub>p</sub>ulation <sub>p</sub>olic<sub>y</sub> [36] or b<sub>y</sub> reasonin<sub>g</sub> with vision-lan<sub>g</sub>ua<sub>g</sub>e models [30, 44]. These methods out<sub>p</sub>ut candidate <sub>p</sub>oses, afordance re<sub>g</sub>ions, or <sub>p</sub>lacement scores for a s<sub>p</sub>ecific robot, policy, or scene. None of them supervises the approach trajectory that leads to the chosen pose. ## 3 Method

This section first defines the MAP and describes how MAP-Data and MAP-Bench are built (Sec. 3.1). It then <sub>p</sub>resents U<sub>n</sub>iWAM<sub>,</sub> i<sub>nc</sub>l<sub>u</sub>di<sub>ng</sub> it<sub>s s</sub>t<sub>ream</sub> i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>er</sub>f<sub>aces, m</sub>i<sub>xe</sub>d<sub>-s</sub>t<sub>ream</sub> b<sub>a</sub>t<sub>c</sub>h <sub>pac</sub>ki<sub>ng,</sub> i<sub>n</sub>d<sub>epen</sub>d<sub>en</sub>t <sub>s</sub>t<sub>ream samp</sub>li<sub>ng,</sub> i<sub>mage-p</sub>l<sub>ane</sub> auxiliar<sub>y</sub> su<sub>p</sub>ervision, trainin<sub>g</sub> objective, and im<sub>p</sub>lementation (Sec. 3.2).

## 3.1 MAP: Manipulation Anchor Pose

E<sub>x</sub>i<sub>s</sub>ti<sub>ng represen</sub>t<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>ons o</sub>f <sub>man</sub>i<sub>pu</sub>l<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>on-rea</sub>d<sub>y</sub> b<sub>ase p</sub>l<sub>acemen</sub>t <sub>are spec</sub>ifi<sub>c</sub> t<sub>o a ro</sub>b<sub>o</sub>t<sub>, po</sub>li<sub>cy, or scene an</sub>d d<sub>o no</sub>t su<sub>p</sub>ervise the a<sub>pp</sub>roach trajector<sub>y</sub> [6, 43]. We instead make the terminal base <sub>p</sub>ose an ex<sub>p</sub>licit tar<sub>g</sub>et and <sub>g</sub>enerate approach trajectories toward it at scale, which yields dense supervision for every step of the approach.

MAP definition. Stage S2 constructs the MAP of each target and a set of feasible observation <sub>poses.</sub> A <sub>v</sub>i<sub>s</sub>i<sub>on-</sub>l<sub>anguage mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>l id<sub>en</sub>tifi<sub>es</sub> th<sub>e man</sub>i<sub>pu</sub>l<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>on-</sub>f<sub>ac</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> f<sub>ron</sub>t <sub>e</sub>d<sub>ge o</sub>f th<sub>e</sub> t<sub>arge</sub>t<sub>.</sub> L<sub>e</sub>t $\mathbf { p } _ { f }$ b<sub>e</sub> th<sub>e groun</sub>d<sub>-p</sub>l<sub>ane</sub> <sub>m</sub>id<sub>po</sub>i<sub>n</sub>t <sub>o</sub>f thi<sub>s</sub> <sub>e</sub>d<sub>ge</sub> <sub>an</sub>d ${ \bf n } _ { f }$ it<sub>s</sub> <sub>un</sub>it <sub>norma</sub>l <sub>po</sub>i<sub>n</sub>ti<sub>ng</sub> t<sub>owar</sub>d <sub>nav</sub>i<sub>ga</sub>bl<sub>e</sub> <sub>space.</sub> D<sub>eno</sub>ti<sub>ng</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>c</sub>l<sub>earance-aware</sub> <sub>nav</sub>i<sub>ga</sub>bl<sub>e</sub> re<sub>g</sub><sup>i</sup>on <sup>b</sup><sub>y</sub> $\mathcal { F } _ { \mathrm { n a v } }$ <sub>,</sub> th<sub>e</sub> MAP <sub>pos</sub>iti<sub>on</sub> <sub>an</sub>d h<sub>ea</sub>di<sub>ng</sub> <sub>are</sub>

$$
\mathbf { p } ^ { \mathrm { M A P } } = \mathrm { R a y } ( \mathbf { p } _ { f } , \mathbf { n } _ { f } ) \cap \partial \mathcal { F } _ { \mathrm { n a v } } , \qquad \theta ^ { \mathrm { M A P } } = \mathrm { a t a n 2 } \Big ( ( \mathbf { p } _ { f } - \mathbf { p } ^ { \mathrm { M A P } } ) _ { \mathrm { y } } , ( \mathbf { p } _ { f } - \mathbf { p } ^ { \mathrm { M A P } } ) _ { x } \Big ) .\tag{1}
$$

Trajectory synthesis. Stage S3 connects each retained observation pose to its MAP by a single-segment or multi-segment cubic Bezier trajectory. A segment with control points´ $\{ \mathbf { c } _ { j } \} _ { j = 0 } ^ { 3 }$ i<sub>s</sub>

$$
\mathbf { q } ( s ) = ( 1 - s ) ^ { 3 } \mathbf { c } _ { 0 } + 3 ( 1 - s ) ^ { 2 } s \mathbf { c } _ { 1 } + 3 ( 1 - s ) s ^ { 2 } \mathbf { c } _ { 2 } + s ^ { 3 } \mathbf { c } _ { 3 } , \qquad s \in [ 0 , 1 ] .\tag{2}
$$

A trajector<sub>y</sub> is retained <sub>on</sub>l<sub>y</sub> if it <sub>rema</sub>i<sub>ns w</sub>ithi<sub>n</sub> th<sub>e c</sub>l<sub>earance-aware nav</sub>i<sub>ga</sub>ti<sub>on mas</sub>k<sub>, approac</sub>h<sub>es</sub> th<sub>e</sub> t<sub>arge</sub>t f<sub>rom</sub> th<sub>e anno</sub>t<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d <sub>s</sub>id<sub>e,</sub> k<sub>eeps</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>man</sub>i<sub>pu</sub>l<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>on-</sub>f<sub>ac</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> b<sub>oun</sub>d<sub>ary w</sub>ithi<sub>n</sub> th<sub>e</sub> h<sub>or</sub>i<sub>zon</sub>t<sub>a</sub>l fi<sub>e</sub>ld <sub>o</sub>f <sub>v</sub>i<sub>ew, an</sub>d <sub>sa</sub>ti<sub>s</sub>fi<sub>es curva</sub>t<sub>ure an</sub>d <sub>pa</sub>th<sub>-</sub>l<sub>eng</sub>th <sub>cons</sub>t<sub>ra</sub>i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>s.</sub> Trajectories with excessive backtracking or an inaccurate terminal position or heading are rejected.

Synchronized geometric labels. At each trajectory step, the renderer records the egocentric RGB observation and the planar navigation action. The MAP position is projected into the current camera as

$$
\mathbf { u } _ { t } ^ { \mathrm { M A P } } = \Pi ( T _ { c _ { t }  w } [ x _ { \mathrm { M A P } } \quad y _ { \mathrm { M A P } } \quad z _ { \mathrm { M A P } } \quad 1 ] ^ { \top } ) ,\tag{3}
$$

<sub>w</sub>h<sub>ere</sub> $T _ { c _ { t } \gets w }$ ma<sub>p</sub>s world coordinates to the camera frame and Π a<sub>pp</sub>lies the camera intrinsics and <sub>p</sub>ers<sub>p</sub>ective division. Target-instance masks are converted to image-plane bounding boxes. RGB frames, navigation actions, projected MAP <sub>coor</sub>di<sub>na</sub>t<sub>es,</sub> t<sub>arge</sub>t b<sub>oxes, scene an</sub>d t<sub>arge</sub>t id<sub>en</sub>tifi<sub>ers, an</sub>d <sub>qua</sub>lit<sub>y</sub> fl<sub>ags are s</sub>t<sub>ore</sub>d <sub>w</sub>ith th<sub>e same</sub> ti<sub>mes</sub>t<sub>amps</sub> f<sub>or</sub> frame-level alignment. Each trajectory also carries five target-specific instructions. MAP-Data contains over 1.5M episodes and 7.5K hours of navigation trajectories with explicit MAP and image-plane geometric supervision.

MAP-Bench. MAP-Bench contains 300 targets in 109 scenes. Its training split contains 35,454 trajectories from 6,732 observation poses, with 7,184,098 RGB frames at 15 Hz, and its evaluation split contains 15K trajectories. ## 3.2.3 Independent Stream Sampling

In a complete mobile-manipulation trajectory, the arm is mostly idle during navigation and the base is mostly idle during manipulation. We therefore split each mobile-manipulation trajectory by rule into navigation windows and manipulation <sub>w</sub>i<sub>n</sub>d<sub>ows, one se</sub>t f<sub>or eac</sub>h <sub>s</sub>t<sub>ream.</sub> Withi<sub>n eac</sub>h <sub>s</sub>t<sub>ream, a secon</sub>d <sub>ru</sub>l<sub>e mar</sub>k<sub>s every w</sub>i<sub>n</sub>d<sub>ow as ac</sub>ti<sub>ve or</sub> idl<sub>e,</sub> d<sub>epen</sub>di<sub>ng</sub> <sub>on w</sub>h<sub>e</sub>th<sub>er</sub> th<sub>e con</sub>t<sub>ro</sub>ll<sub>e</sub>d <sub>par</sub>t <sub>o</sub>f th<sub>e ro</sub>b<sub>o</sub>t <sub>moves.</sub> E<sub>ac</sub>h <sub>w</sub>i<sub>n</sub>d<sub>ow</sub> th<sub>en</sub> b<sub>ecomes a separa</sub>t<sub>e examp</sub>l<sub>e o</sub>f it<sub>s s</sub>t<sub>ream.</sub> A mobile-manipulation trajectory thus contributes to both streams, whereas navigation-only data such as MAP-Data and <sub>man</sub>i<sub>pu</sub>l<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>on-on</sub>l<sub>y</sub> d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>a con</sub>t<sub>r</sub>ib<sub>u</sub>t<sub>e on</sub>l<sub>y</sub> t<sub>o</sub> th<sub>e</sub>i<sub>r own s</sub>t<sub>ream.</sub> E<sub>xamp</sub>l<sub>es are</sub> d<sub>rawn</sub> f<sub>rom</sub>

B<sub>o</sub>th <sub>s</sub>t<sub>reams</sub> <sub>pre</sub>di<sub>c</sub>t <sub>spa</sub>ti<sub>a</sub>l <sub>ac</sub>ti<sub>ons</sub> f<sub>rom</sub> i<sub>mages.</sub> T<sub>o</sub> <sub>groun</sub>d th<sub>ese</sub> <sub>ac</sub>ti<sub>ons</sub> i<sub>n</sub> th<sub>e</sub> i<sub>mage,</sub> <sub>we</sub> <sub>appen</sub>d i<sub>mage-p</sub>l<sub>ane</sub> t<sub>arge</sub>t<sub>s</sub> t<sub>o eac</sub>h <sub>ac</sub>ti<sub>on vec</sub>t<sub>or,</sub>

$$
a _ { t , i } ^ { s } = \big [ a _ { t , i } ^ { s , \mathrm { a c t } } ; a _ { t , i } ^ { s , \mathrm { a u x } } \big ] ,\tag{10}
$$

and denoise them jointly with the actions, as shown in the output row of Fig. 3. For navigation, the targets are the target bounding box and the projected MAP position of Eq. 3, which indicate where the target is and where the base should stop at every step of the approach. For manipulation, the targets are the two end-efector trajectories projected into the main camera view, together with visibility flags. Predicting these tracks jointly with the camera-frame end-efector poses explicitly links the camera-frame actions to their image-plane locations in the main view. $$

$$

UniWAM variants. Both variants start from Wan2.2-TI2V-5B [27] without embodied pretraining. UniWAM-Base uses only the data available to the baselines and is the matched comparison. It is trained on the 35K-trajectory training split on MAP-Bench and on the demonstrations of the 24 evaluation tasks on real robots. ## 4.7 Failure Cases

Grasping between adjacent objects. Fig. 10 shows a failure of UniWAM in the color-sorting task.
